From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for things has gone really quiet. Like reaching out to people, wanting to see friends, or feeling anything satisfying from work — and I used to care a lot about my work, maybe too much sometimes. I’m a social worker, so connection was sort of built into who I thought I was.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I used to do yoga, go to galleries, write in my journal — not necessarily every day, but those things felt like they gave me something back. Lately I’m mostly just… moving through obligations. I do what I have to do, but there’s this flatness around it. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, because I’m still functioning, I think. But I don’t feel pulled toward people or things the way I used to, and that’s what scared me enough to make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, I think. It didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I look back, it started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. Since then, the way I think, the way I work, the way I connect with people… all of that has gradually shifted.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, because having another person in the apartment changed the rhythm of everything. But it’s gone on long enough now that I can’t really explain it away as just being tired or needing a few weeks to adapt. So, yes — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has that changed as well?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. That’s probably one of the clearest parts of it, even though it took me a while to admit that’s what was happening.\n\nI still do some things, technically. I still go through the motions. But the feeling I used to get from them is just… not really there. Work used to give me a sense of meaning, even on hard days. I could feel connected to people, feel like I was useful, like something mattered. Now I mostly just complete what needs to be completed.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s similar. Yoga used to help me feel grounded, galleries used to make me curious, journaling used to help me sort myself out. Now I can look at those things and know, intellectually, that I used to enjoy them, but I don’t feel pulled toward them. It’s like the part of me that would reach for people or activities because they gave me something back has gone very quiet.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say the pleasure has changed a lot. I’m not completely unable to do things, but I mostly feel like I’m moving through obligations rather than actually wanting or enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you've experienced such a long period of feeling flat and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve had periods before where I was tired or kind of burned out — especially with work, because the work can take a lot out of you — but this feels different in its length and in the way it’s sort of spread into everything.\n\nBefore, if I was worn down, I could usually still find something that pulled me back a little. A yoga class, or going to see something at a gallery, or writing things out in my journal, or even just talking with someone I trusted. There was still some part of me that wanted to reach for those things.\n\nNow that part feels very quiet. That’s really what scared me enough to come in. It isn’t just that I’m having a bad week or that I’m not in the mood for one activity. It’s like the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling — all of that — has sort of dimmed. I’m still doing what I have to do, but mostly it feels like I’m moving through obligations rather than actually wanting or enjoying things.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had such a long stretch of feeling this flat and disconnected before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now, with more energy and interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, definitely, when I compare it to how I was before this. I wouldn’t say I was constantly energetic or anything — my work has always taken a lot emotionally — but there used to be more of a natural pull toward things.\n\nI used to want to connect with people. I used to care more, not just in the professional sense but in a real internal way. Work could be draining, but it also gave me something back. Yoga helped me feel settled. Going to galleries made me feel curious and awake. Even journaling — I used to reach for it because it helped me understand myself.\n\nNow it’s more like… I know those things belong to me, or used to belong to me, but the feeling behind them is much quieter. That’s really what finally brought me in. It feels like the part of me that used to reach outward — toward people, toward work, toward the things I enjoyed — has gone very still, and I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where you felt depressed for a shorter period of time, let's say less than a few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I can think of times when I felt low for a while, but I don’t know if I would call them “episodes,” exactly.\n\nThere have been stretches in my life where I was sad, or drained, or disappointed — sometimes for a few weeks, maybe around stressful periods — but I always had some sense of why it was happening, and it felt more contained. Like, if work was especially heavy or something in my personal life was weighing on me, I might feel down for a bit, but it didn’t feel like this broader kind of flattening.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I’ve had shorter periods of feeling depressed in the ordinary sense of the word. But nothing that stands out to me as a clear, separate episode the way you’re asking, and nothing that felt as prolonged or as hard to shake as what’s been happening now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s very muted. I keep trying to test it, almost — like, if I go to a yoga class, or if I stop into a gallery, or if I sit down with my journal, maybe something will click back on. And sometimes I can recognize that something is nice, in a very intellectual way. Like, “This painting is beautiful,” or “My body probably needed that stretch,” or “Writing this down is sensible.” But the actual pleasure part feels very faint.\n\nWork is similar. I still care about doing it properly, and I still show up, but I don’t feel the same sense of connection or meaning from it that I used to. And with people, too — I know I should reach out, and sometimes I do, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than being pulled toward them.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me come in. It’s not that I can’t name activities I used to enjoy. I can. It’s that the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, the whole thing — the change in how I’m thinking, how I’m functioning at work, how I’m connecting with people. It all sort of started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment.\n\nWith sleep specifically… I’m trying to separate that out, because the timing all blends together in my head. But when I think about the pattern, that eight-month period is the frame I keep coming back to. Before that, even if I was tired or stressed, I felt more like myself. Since then, things have just felt different in this ongoing way, not like a bad week and then it lifts. It’s been sustained since they moved in with us.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not in a way that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully, and I’m not someone who weighs myself every day or anything like that, so I could be missing small changes. But my clothes fit basically the same, as far as I can tell, and nobody has said anything to me about looking like I’ve lost or gained weight.\n\nIf there’s been any fluctuation, it’s probably within the usual range for me. I can’t point to anything significant over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of guilt or worthlessness over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I can be hard on myself — I think I’ve always had some of that, especially around work or whether I’m being present enough for people — but I don’t know that I’ve had this big, overwhelming guilt, like I did something terrible, or a strong feeling that I’m worthless.\n\nIt’s more… I can get frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll think, “Why can’t I just be more engaged?” or “Why does everything feel so effortful?” But that feels different from guilt, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m lying awake cataloging all the ways I’ve failed everyone.\n\nWorthlessness… no, I don’t think so. I don’t feel great about myself lately, but I can still sort of intellectually know I have value, even if I’m not feeling very connected to it emotionally. So I guess my answer is: not significantly, no. At least not in the way I understand those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little interest in activities, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two years. It feels like it’s been about eight months, give or take — that’s the period I keep coming back to when I try to put a timeline on it.\n\nThe change in my interest in things, and really the broader change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people, seems to have started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Before that, I had normal ups and downs, but it wasn’t this sustained thing. So I’d say it’s been months, not years — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I felt low or discouraged for more than a couple of weeks, sure, but I don’t know if I would call it “depressed” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nLike, there have been stressful stretches where I was sad, worn down, not myself, but I don’t know that I can point to a clear episode and say, yes, that was definitely depression for more than two weeks. I tend to kind of keep functioning and second-guess whether what I’m feeling “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe, but I’m not confident. I don’t have a specific time that stands out to me as clearly being that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that with confidence. I mean, I’ve had different stretches in my life where I was down, or stressed, or sort of emotionally worn out, but when you ask “more than one period where you felt depressed,” I start wondering what threshold you mean.\n\nIf you mean, like, clear separate episodes where I would have recognized at the time, “I am depressed,” then… I’m not sure. I don’t have two distinct chapters in my mind that I can point to that way. It’s more like there have been times when life was hard and I didn’t feel like myself, but I don’t know whether those were really depression or just reactions to circumstances.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I can’t reliably identify more than one clear period. I’m hesitant to say yes when I’m not sure I’m using the word the way you are.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a previous episode of depression that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. I’m trying to think back, and I’ve definitely had times where I was unhappy or under a lot of strain for a couple weeks or longer, but whether that was a real “episode of depression” the way you’re asking… I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a prior period where I would say, yes, that was definitely depression for at least two weeks. It’s more that I can remember being worn down or discouraged at different points, but I’m hesitant to label it because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought you might be depressed before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve wondered at times whether I was just really unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember thinking, “I’m depressed,” like as a definite thing.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to analyze it too much. I’ll think, well, I’m tired, I’m discouraged, things feel heavy—but then I’ll also tell myself there are reasons for feeling that way, or that maybe I’m just stressed. So before this period, I don’t think I had a firm sense that depression was what was happening. Maybe the thought crossed my mind vaguely, but I can’t say I really identified it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

No, not that I can recall. I don’t remember a healthcare professional ever telling me, before this current period, “you have depression” or putting that diagnosis on it in a clear way.

I used to do yoga, go to galleries, write in my journal — not necessarily every day, but those things felt like they gave me something back. Lately I’m mostly just… moving through obligations. It didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I look back, it started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. But it’s gone on long enough now that I can’t really explain it away as just being tired or needing a few weeks to adapt. So, yes — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has that changed as well?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. That’s probably one of the clearest parts of it, even though it took me a while to admit that’s what was happening.\n\nI still do some things, technically. I still go through the motions. Yoga used to help me feel grounded, galleries used to make me curious, journaling used to help me sort myself out. Now I can look at those things and know, intellectually, that I used to enjoy them, but I don’t feel pulled toward them. It’s like the part of me that would reach for people or activities because they gave me something back has gone very quiet.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say the pleasure has changed a lot. I’m not completely unable to do things, but I mostly feel like I’m moving through obligations rather than actually wanting or enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you've experienced such a long period of feeling flat and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve had periods before where I was tired or kind of burned out — especially with work, because the work can take a lot out of you — but this feels different in its length and in the way it’s sort of spread into everything.\n\nBefore, if I was worn down, I could usually still find something that pulled me back a little. A yoga class, or going to see something at a gallery, or writing things out in my journal, or even just talking with someone I trusted. There was still some part of me that wanted to reach for those things.\n\nNow that part feels very quiet. That’s really what scared me enough to come in. It’s like the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling — all of that — has sort of dimmed. I’m still doing what I have to do, but mostly it feels like I’m moving through obligations rather than actually wanting or enjoying things.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had such a long stretch of feeling this flat and disconnected before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now, with more energy and interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, definitely, when I compare it to how I was before this. I wouldn’t say I was constantly energetic or anything — my work has always taken a lot emotionally — but there used to be more of a natural pull toward things.\n\nI used to want to connect with people. I used to care more, not just in the professional sense but in a real internal way. Work could be draining, but it also gave me something back. Yoga helped me feel settled. Going to galleries made me feel curious and awake. That’s really what finally brought me in. It feels like the part of me that used to reach outward — toward people, toward work, toward the things I enjoyed — has gone very still, and I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where you felt depressed for a shorter period of time, let's say less than a few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I can think of times when I felt low for a while, but I don’t know if I would call them “episodes,” exactly.\n\nThere have been stretches in my life where I was sad, or drained, or disappointed — sometimes for a few weeks, maybe around stressful periods — but I always had some sense of why it was happening, and it felt more contained. Like, if work was especially heavy or something in my personal life was weighing on me, I might feel down for a bit, but it didn’t feel like this broader kind of flattening.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I’ve had shorter periods of feeling depressed in the ordinary sense of the word. But nothing that stands out to me as a clear, separate episode the way you’re asking, and nothing that felt as prolonged or as hard to shake as what’s been happening now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s very muted. I keep trying to test it, almost — like, if I go to a yoga class, or if I stop into a gallery, or if I sit down with my journal, maybe something will click back on. And sometimes I can recognize that something is nice, in a very intellectual way. Like, “This painting is beautiful,” or “My body probably needed that stretch,” or “Writing this down is sensible.” But the actual pleasure part feels very faint.\n\nWork is similar. I still care about doing it properly, and I still show up, but I don’t feel the same sense of connection or meaning from it that I used to. And with people, too — I know I should reach out, and sometimes I do, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than being pulled toward them.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me come in. It’s not that I can’t name activities I used to enjoy. I can. It’s that the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, the whole thing — the change in how I’m thinking, how I’m functioning at work, how I’m connecting with people. It all sort of started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment.\n\nWith sleep specifically… I’m trying to separate that out, because the timing all blends together in my head. But when I think about the pattern, that eight-month period is the frame I keep coming back to. Before that, even if I was tired or stressed, I felt more like myself. Since then, things have just felt different in this ongoing way, not like a bad week and then it lifts. It’s been sustained since they moved in with us.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not in a way that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully, and I’m not someone who weighs myself every day or anything like that, so I could be missing small changes. But my clothes fit basically the same, as far as I can tell, and nobody has said anything to me about looking like I’ve lost or gained weight.\n\nIf there’s been any fluctuation, it’s probably within the usual range for me. I can’t point to anything significant over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of guilt or worthlessness over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I can be hard on myself — I think I’ve always had some of that, especially around work or whether I’m being present enough for people — but I don’t know that I’ve had this big, overwhelming guilt, like I did something terrible, or a strong feeling that I’m worthless.\n\nIt’s more… I can get frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll think, “Why can’t I just be more engaged?” or “Why does everything feel so effortful?” But that feels different from guilt, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m lying awake cataloging all the ways I’ve failed everyone.\n\nWorthlessness… no, I don’t think so. I don’t feel great about myself lately, but I can still sort of intellectually know I have value, even if I’m not feeling very connected to it emotionally. So I guess my answer is: not significantly, no. Before that, I had normal ups and downs, but it wasn’t this sustained thing. I tend to kind of keep functioning and second-guess whether what I’m feeling “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe, but I’m not confident. I don’t have a specific time that stands out to me as clearly being that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that with confidence. I mean, I’ve had periods before where I was tired or kind of burned out — especially with work, because the work can take a lot out of you — but this feels different in its length and in the way it’s sort of spread into everything.\n\nBefore, if I was worn down, I could usually still find something that pulled me back a little. A yoga class, or going to see something at a gallery, or writing things out in my journal, or even just talking with someone I trusted. There was still some part of me that wanted to reach for those things.\n\nNow that part feels very quiet. That’s really what scared me enough to come in. It isn’t just that I’m having a bad week or that I’m not in the mood for one activity. It’s like the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling — all of that — has sort of dimmed. I’m still doing what I have to do, but mostly it feels like I’m moving through obligations rather than actually wanting or enjoying things.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had such a long stretch of feeling this flat and disconnected before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now, with more energy and interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, definitely, when I compare it to how I was before this. I wouldn’t say I was constantly energetic or anything — my work has always taken a lot emotionally — but there used to be more of a natural pull toward things.\n\nI used to want to connect with people. I used to care more, not just in the professional sense but in a real internal way. Work could be draining, but it also gave me something back. Yoga helped me feel settled. Going to galleries made me feel curious and awake. Even journaling — I used to reach for it because it helped me understand myself.\n\nNow it’s more like… I know those things belong to me, or used to belong to me, but the feeling behind them is much quieter. That’s really what finally brought me in. It feels like the part of me that used to reach outward — toward people, toward work, toward the things I enjoyed — has gone very still, and I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where you felt depressed for a shorter period of time, let's say less than a few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I can think of times when I felt low for a while, but I don’t know if I would call them “episodes,” exactly.\n\nThere have been stretches in my life where I was sad, or drained, or disappointed — sometimes for a few weeks, maybe around stressful periods — but I always had some sense of why it was happening, and it felt more contained. Like, if work was especially heavy or something in my personal life was weighing on me, I might feel down for a bit, but it didn’t feel like this broader kind of flattening.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I’ve had shorter periods of feeling depressed in the ordinary sense of the word. But nothing that stands out to me as a clear, separate episode the way you’re asking, and nothing that felt as prolonged or as hard to shake as what’s been happening now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s very muted. I keep trying to test it, almost — like, if I go to a yoga class, or if I stop into a gallery, or if I sit down with my journal, maybe something will click back on. And sometimes I can recognize that something is nice, in a very intellectual way. Like, “This painting is beautiful,” or “My body probably needed that stretch,” or “Writing this down is sensible.” But the actual pleasure part feels very faint.\n\nWork is similar. I still care about doing it properly, and I still show up, but I don’t feel the same sense of connection or meaning from it that I used to. And with people, too — I know I should reach out, and sometimes I do, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than being pulled toward them.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me come in. It’s not that I can’t name activities I used to enjoy. I can. It’s that the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, the whole thing — the change in how I’m thinking, how I’m functioning at work, how I’m connecting with people. It all sort of started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment.\n\nWith sleep specifically… I’m trying to separate that out, because the timing all blends together in my head. But when I think about the pattern, that eight-month period is the frame I keep coming back to. Before that, even if I was tired or stressed, I felt more like myself. Since then, things have just felt different in this ongoing way, not like a bad week and then it lifts. It’s been sustained since they moved in with us.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not in a way that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully, and I’m not someone who weighs myself every day or anything like that, so I could be missing small changes. But my clothes fit basically the same, as far as I can tell, and nobody has said anything to me about looking like I’ve lost or gained weight.\n\nIf there’s been any fluctuation, it’s probably within the usual range for me. I can’t point to anything significant over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of guilt or worthlessness over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I can be hard on myself — I think I’ve always had some of that, especially around work or whether I’m being present enough for people — but I don’t know that I’ve had this big, overwhelming guilt, like I did something terrible, or a strong feeling that I’m worthless.\n\nIt’s more… I can get frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll think, “Why can’t I just be more engaged?” or “Why does everything feel so effortful?” But that feels different from guilt, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m lying awake cataloging all the ways I’ve failed everyone.\n\nWorthlessness… no, I don’t think so. I don’t feel great about myself lately, but I can still sort of intellectually know I have value, even if I’m not feeling very connected to it emotionally. So I guess my answer is: not significantly, no. At least not in the way I understand those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little interest in activities, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two years. It feels like it’s been about eight months, give or take — that’s the period I keep coming back to when I try to put a timeline on it.\n\nThe change in my interest in things, and really the broader change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people, seems to have started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Before that, I had normal ups and downs, but it wasn’t this sustained thing. So I’d say it’s been months, not years — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I felt low or discouraged for more than a couple of weeks, sure, but I don’t know if I would call it “depressed” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nLike, there have been stressful stretches where I was sad, worn down, not myself, but I don’t know that I can point to a clear episode and say, yes, that was definitely depression for more than two weeks. I tend to kind of keep functioning and second-guess whether what I’m feeling “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe, but I’m not confident. I don’t have a specific time that stands out to me as clearly being that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that with confidence. I mean, I’ve had different stretches in my life where I was down, or stressed, or sort of emotionally worn out, but when you ask “more than one period where you felt depressed,” I start wondering what threshold you mean.\n\nIf you mean, like, clear separate episodes where I would have recognized at the time, “I am depressed,” then… I’m not sure. I don’t have two distinct chapters in my mind that I can point to that way. It’s more like there have been times when life was hard and I didn’t feel like myself, but I don’t know whether those were really depression or just reactions to circumstances.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I can’t reliably identify more than one clear period. I’m hesitant to say yes when I’m not sure I’m using the word the way you are.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a previous episode of depression that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. I’m trying to think back, and I’ve definitely had times where I was unhappy or under a lot of strain for a couple weeks or longer, but whether that was a real “episode of depression” the way you’re asking… I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a prior period where I would say, yes, that was definitely depression for at least two weeks. It’s more that I can remember being worn down or discouraged at different points, but I’m hesitant to label it because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought you might be depressed before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve wondered at times whether I was just really unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember thinking, “I’m depressed,” like as a definite thing.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to analyze it too much. I’ll think, well, I’m tired, I’m discouraged, things feel heavy—but then I’ll also tell myself there are reasons for feeling that way, or that maybe I’m just stressed. So before this period, I don’t think I had a firm sense that depression was what was happening. Maybe the thought crossed my mind vaguely, but I can’t say I really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression by a healthcare professional before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. I don’t remember a healthcare professional ever telling me, before this current period, “you have depression” or putting that diagnosis on it in a clear way.\n\nI’ve talked to people in healthcare settings before, but I don’t have a memory of anyone formally diagnosing me with depression. And I’m trying to be careful because there’s a difference between someone saying, you know, “you seem stressed” or asking about mood, versus actually diagnosing depression. So as far as I know, no—I haven’t been diagnosed with depression before this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, “This painting is beautiful,” or “My body probably needed that stretch,” or “Writing this down is sensible.” But the actual pleasure part feels very faint.\n\nWork is similar. I still care about doing it properly, and I still show up, but I don’t feel the same sense of connection or meaning from it that I used to. And with people, too — I know I should reach out, and sometimes I do, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than being pulled toward them.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me come in. It’s not that I can’t name activities I used to enjoy. I can. It’s that the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, the whole thing — the change in how I’m thinking, how I’m functioning at work, how I’m connecting with people. It all sort of started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment.\n\nWith sleep specifically… I’m trying to separate that out, because the timing all blends together in my head. But when I think about the pattern, that eight-month period is the frame I keep coming back to. Before that, even if I was tired or stressed, I felt more like myself. Since then, things have just felt different in this ongoing way, not like a bad week and then it lifts. It’s been sustained since they moved in with us.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not in a way that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully, and I’m not someone who weighs myself every day or anything like that, so I could be missing small changes. But my clothes fit basically the same, as far as I can tell, and nobody has said anything to me about looking like I’ve lost or gained weight.\n\nIf there’s been any fluctuation, it’s probably within the usual range for me. I can’t point to anything significant over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of guilt or worthlessness over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I can be hard on myself — I think I’ve always had some of that, especially around work or whether I’m being present enough for people — but I don’t know that I’ve had this big, overwhelming guilt, like I did something terrible, or a strong feeling that I’m worthless.\n\nIt’s more… I can get frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll think, “Why can’t I just be more engaged?” or “Why does everything feel so effortful?” But that feels different from guilt, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m lying awake cataloging all the ways I’ve failed everyone.\n\nWorthlessness… no, I don’t think so. I don’t feel great about myself lately, but I can still sort of intellectually know I have value, even if I’m not feeling very connected to it emotionally. So I guess my answer is: not significantly, no. At least not in the way I understand those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little interest in activities, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two years. It feels like it’s been about eight months, give or take — that’s the period I keep coming back to when I try to put a timeline on it.\n\nThe change in my interest in things, and really the broader change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people, seems to have started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Before that, I had normal ups and downs, but it wasn’t this sustained thing. So I’d say it’s been months, not years — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I felt low or discouraged for more than a couple of weeks, sure, but I don’t know if I would call it “depressed” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nLike, there have been stressful stretches where I was sad, worn down, not myself, but I don’t know that I can point to a clear episode and say, yes, that was definitely depression for more than two weeks. I tend to kind of keep functioning and second-guess whether what I’m feeling “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe, but I’m not confident. I don’t have a specific time that stands out to me as clearly being that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that with confidence. I mean, I’ve had different stretches in my life where I was down, or stressed, or sort of emotionally worn out, but when you ask “more than one period where you felt depressed,” I start wondering what threshold you mean.\n\nIf you mean, like, clear separate episodes where I would have recognized at the time, “I am depressed,” then… I’m not sure. I don’t have two distinct chapters in my mind that I can point to that way. It’s more like there have been times when life was hard and I didn’t feel like myself, but I don’t know whether those were really depression or just reactions to circumstances.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I can’t reliably identify more than one clear period. I’m hesitant to say yes when I’m not sure I’m using the word the way you are.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a previous episode of depression that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. I’m trying to think back, and I’ve definitely had times where I was unhappy or under a lot of strain for a couple weeks or longer, but whether that was a real “episode of depression” the way you’re asking… I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a prior period where I would say, yes, that was definitely depression for at least two weeks. It’s more that I can remember being worn down or discouraged at different points, but I’m hesitant to label it because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought you might be depressed before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve wondered at times whether I was just really unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember thinking, “I’m depressed,” like as a definite thing.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to analyze it too much. I’ll think, well, I’m tired, I’m discouraged, things feel heavy—but then I’ll also tell myself there are reasons for feeling that way, or that maybe I’m just stressed. So before this period, I don’t think I had a firm sense that depression was what was happening. Maybe the thought crossed my mind vaguely, but I can’t say I really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression by a healthcare professional before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. I don’t remember a healthcare professional ever telling me, before this current period, “you have depression” or putting that diagnosis on it in a clear way.\n\nI’ve talked to people in healthcare settings before, but I don’t have a memory of anyone formally diagnosing me with depression. And I’m trying to be careful because there’s a difference between someone saying, you know, “you seem stressed” or asking about mood, versus actually diagnosing depression. So as far as I know, no—I haven’t been diagnosed with depression before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant depression before the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Before the past eight months… I don’t think I can point to anything that I would call significant depression, no. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches in life, like anyone does, and sometimes I was very tired or discouraged or just kind of emotionally weighed down, but I don’t remember a distinct period before this where it felt clearly like a major depressive period.\n\nI’m trying to answer carefully because I don’t want to say “no” as if I’ve never had sadness or stress, but if you mean a period that really stood out as significant depression, before these last eight months, I’m not aware of one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizophrenia, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever thought you might be depressed before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve wondered at times whether I was just really unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember thinking, “I’m depressed,” like as a definite thing.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to analyze it too much. I’ll think, well, I’m tired, I’m discouraged, things feel heavy—but then I’ll also tell myself there are reasons for feeling that way, or that maybe I’m just stressed. So before this period, I don’t think I had a firm sense that depression was what was happening. Maybe the thought crossed my mind vaguely, but I can’t say I really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression by a healthcare professional before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. I don’t remember a healthcare professional ever telling me, before this current period, “you have depression” or putting that diagnosis on it in a clear way.\n\nI’ve talked to people in healthcare settings before, but I don’t have a memory of anyone formally diagnosing me with depression. And I’m trying to be careful because there’s a difference between someone saying, you know, “you seem stressed” or asking about mood, versus actually diagnosing depression. So as far as I know, no—I haven’t been diagnosed with depression before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant depression before the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Before the past eight months… I don’t think I can point to anything that I would call significant depression, no. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches in life, like anyone does, and sometimes I was very tired or discouraged or just kind of emotionally weighed down, but I don’t remember a distinct period before this where it felt clearly like a major depressive period.\n\nI’m trying to answer carefully because I don’t want to say “no” as if I’ve never had sadness or stress, but if you mean a period that really stood out as significant depression, before these last eight months, I’m not aware of one.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any previous episodes of depression that were treated and resolved before this current period started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I can’t remember any earlier episode where I was treated specifically for depression and then it resolved before this current period started.

I mean, I’ve had times where I talked things through with people, or tried to get support in a general way, but not something I would describe as, “I had a depressive episode, it was treated, and then it got better.” That doesn’t really fit my memory of things. If there was anything like that, I don’t have a clear recollection of it, and I’d be hesitant to say yes just to fill in the blank. So as far as I know, no.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for things has gone really quiet. Like reaching out to people, wanting to see friends, or feeling anything satisfying from work — and I used to care a lot about my work, maybe too much sometimes. I’m a social worker, so connection was sort of built into who I thought I was.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I used to do yoga, go to galleries, write in my journal — not necessarily every day, but those things felt like they gave me something back. Lately I’m mostly just… moving through obligations. I do what I have to do, but there’s this flatness around it. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, because I’m still functioning, I think. But I don’t feel pulled toward people or things the way I used to, and that’s what scared me enough to make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, I think. It didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I look back, it started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. Since then, the way I think, the way I work, the way I connect with people… all of that has gradually shifted.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, because having another person in the apartment changed the rhythm of everything. But it’s gone on long enough now that I can’t really explain it away as just being tired or needing a few weeks to adapt. So, yes — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has that changed as well?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. That’s probably one of the clearest parts of it, even though it took me a while to admit that’s what was happening.\n\nI still do some things, technically. I still go through the motions. But the feeling I used to get from them is just… not really there. Work used to give me a sense of meaning, even on hard days. I could feel connected to people, feel like I was useful, like something mattered. Now I mostly just complete what needs to be completed.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s similar. Yoga used to help me feel grounded, galleries used to make me curious, journaling used to help me sort myself out. Now I can look at those things and know, intellectually, that I used to enjoy them, but I don’t feel pulled toward them. It’s like the part of me that would reach for people or activities because they gave me something back has gone very quiet.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say the pleasure has changed a lot. I’m not completely unable to do things, but I mostly feel like I’m moving through obligations rather than actually wanting or enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you've experienced such a long period of feeling flat and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve had periods before where I was tired or kind of burned out — especially with work, because the work can take a lot out of you — but this feels different in its length and in the way it’s sort of spread into everything.\n\nBefore, if I was worn down, I could usually still find something that pulled me back a little. A yoga class, or going to see something at a gallery, or writing things out in my journal, or even just talking with someone I trusted. There was still some part of me that wanted to reach for those things.\n\nNow that part feels very quiet. That’s really what scared me enough to come in. It isn’t just that I’m having a bad week or that I’m not in the mood for one activity. It’s like the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, journaling — all of that — has sort of dimmed. I’m still doing what I have to do, but mostly it feels like I’m moving through obligations rather than actually wanting or enjoying things.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had such a long stretch of feeling this flat and disconnected before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now, with more energy and interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, definitely, when I compare it to how I was before this. I wouldn’t say I was constantly energetic or anything — my work has always taken a lot emotionally — but there used to be more of a natural pull toward things.\n\nI used to want to connect with people. I used to care more, not just in the professional sense but in a real internal way. Work could be draining, but it also gave me something back. Yoga helped me feel settled. Going to galleries made me feel curious and awake. Even journaling — I used to reach for it because it helped me understand myself.\n\nNow it’s more like… I know those things belong to me, or used to belong to me, but the feeling behind them is much quieter. That’s really what finally brought me in. It feels like the part of me that used to reach outward — toward people, toward work, toward the things I enjoyed — has gone very still, and I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where you felt depressed for a shorter period of time, let's say less than a few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I can think of times when I felt low for a while, but I don’t know if I would call them “episodes,” exactly.\n\nThere have been stretches in my life where I was sad, or drained, or disappointed — sometimes for a few weeks, maybe around stressful periods — but I always had some sense of why it was happening, and it felt more contained. Like, if work was especially heavy or something in my personal life was weighing on me, I might feel down for a bit, but it didn’t feel like this broader kind of flattening.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I’ve had shorter periods of feeling depressed in the ordinary sense of the word. But nothing that stands out to me as a clear, separate episode the way you’re asking, and nothing that felt as prolonged or as hard to shake as what’s been happening now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s very muted. I keep trying to test it, almost — like, if I go to a yoga class, or if I stop into a gallery, or if I sit down with my journal, maybe something will click back on. And sometimes I can recognize that something is nice, in a very intellectual way. Like, “This painting is beautiful,” or “My body probably needed that stretch,” or “Writing this down is sensible.” But the actual pleasure part feels very faint.\n\nWork is similar. I still care about doing it properly, and I still show up, but I don’t feel the same sense of connection or meaning from it that I used to. And with people, too — I know I should reach out, and sometimes I do, but it feels more like I’m completing a task than being pulled toward them.\n\nThat’s really what finally made me come in. It’s not that I can’t name activities I used to enjoy. I can. It’s that the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling, has gone very quiet. I’m mostly just moving through obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: It’s been about eight months, the whole thing — the change in how I’m thinking, how I’m functioning at work, how I’m connecting with people. It all sort of started around the time we took my older relative into our apartment.\n\nWith sleep specifically… I’m trying to separate that out, because the timing all blends together in my head. But when I think about the pattern, that eight-month period is the frame I keep coming back to. Before that, even if I was tired or stressed, I felt more like myself. Since then, things have just felt different in this ongoing way, not like a bad week and then it lifts. It’s been sustained since they moved in with us.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not in a way that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully, and I’m not someone who weighs myself every day or anything like that, so I could be missing small changes. But my clothes fit basically the same, as far as I can tell, and nobody has said anything to me about looking like I’ve lost or gained weight.\n\nIf there’s been any fluctuation, it’s probably within the usual range for me. I can’t point to anything significant over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of guilt or worthlessness over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I can be hard on myself — I think I’ve always had some of that, especially around work or whether I’m being present enough for people — but I don’t know that I’ve had this big, overwhelming guilt, like I did something terrible, or a strong feeling that I’m worthless.\n\nIt’s more… I can get frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll think, “Why can’t I just be more engaged?” or “Why does everything feel so effortful?” But that feels different from guilt, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m lying awake cataloging all the ways I’ve failed everyone.\n\nWorthlessness… no, I don’t think so. I don’t feel great about myself lately, but I can still sort of intellectually know I have value, even if I’m not feeling very connected to it emotionally. So I guess my answer is: not significantly, no. At least not in the way I understand those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little interest in activities, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two years. It feels like it’s been about eight months, give or take — that’s the period I keep coming back to when I try to put a timeline on it.\n\nThe change in my interest in things, and really the broader change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people, seems to have started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Before that, I had normal ups and downs, but it wasn’t this sustained thing. So I’d say it’s been months, not years — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I felt low or discouraged for more than a couple of weeks, sure, but I don’t know if I would call it “depressed” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nLike, there have been stressful stretches where I was sad, worn down, not myself, but I don’t know that I can point to a clear episode and say, yes, that was definitely depression for more than two weeks. I tend to kind of keep functioning and second-guess whether what I’m feeling “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe, but I’m not confident. I don’t have a specific time that stands out to me as clearly being that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that with confidence. I mean, I’ve had different stretches in my life where I was down, or stressed, or sort of emotionally worn out, but when you ask “more than one period where you felt depressed,” I start wondering what threshold you mean.\n\nIf you mean, like, clear separate episodes where I would have recognized at the time, “I am depressed,” then… I’m not sure. I don’t have two distinct chapters in my mind that I can point to that way. It’s more like there have been times when life was hard and I didn’t feel like myself, but I don’t know whether those were really depression or just reactions to circumstances.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I can’t reliably identify more than one clear period. I’m hesitant to say yes when I’m not sure I’m using the word the way you are.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a previous episode of depression that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. I’m trying to think back, and I’ve definitely had times where I was unhappy or under a lot of strain for a couple weeks or longer, but whether that was a real “episode of depression” the way you’re asking… I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a prior period where I would say, yes, that was definitely depression for at least two weeks. It’s more that I can remember being worn down or discouraged at different points, but I’m hesitant to label it because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought you might be depressed before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve wondered at times whether I was just really unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember thinking, “I’m depressed,” like as a definite thing.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to analyze it too much. I’ll think, well, I’m tired, I’m discouraged, things feel heavy—but then I’ll also tell myself there are reasons for feeling that way, or that maybe I’m just stressed. So before this period, I don’t think I had a firm sense that depression was what was happening. Maybe the thought crossed my mind vaguely, but I can’t say I really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression by a healthcare professional before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. I don’t remember a healthcare professional ever telling me, before this current period, “you have depression” or putting that diagnosis on it in a clear way.\n\nI’ve talked to people in healthcare settings before, but I don’t have a memory of anyone formally diagnosing me with depression. And I’m trying to be careful because there’s a difference between someone saying, you know, “you seem stressed” or asking about mood, versus actually diagnosing depression. So as far as I know, no—I haven’t been diagnosed with depression before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant depression before the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Before the past eight months… I don’t think I can point to anything that I would call significant depression, no. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches in life, like anyone does, and sometimes I was very tired or discouraged or just kind of emotionally weighed down, but I don’t remember a distinct period before this where it felt clearly like a major depressive period.\n\nI’m trying to answer carefully because I don’t want to say “no” as if I’ve never had sadness or stress, but if you mean a period that really stood out as significant depression, before these last eight months, I’m not aware of one.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any previous episodes of depression that were treated and resolved before this current period started?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can’t remember any earlier episode where I was treated specifically for depression and then it resolved before this current period started.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I talked things through with people, or tried to get support in a general way, but not something I would describe as, “I had a depressive episode, it was treated, and then it got better.” That doesn’t really fit my memory of things. If there was anything like that, I don’t have a clear recollection of it, and I’d be hesitant to say yes just to fill in the blank. So as far as I know, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I can be hard on myself — I think I’ve always had some of that, especially around work or whether I’m being present enough for people — but I don’t know that I’ve had this big, overwhelming guilt, like I did something terrible, or a strong feeling that I’m worthless.\n\nIt’s more… I can get frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll think, “Why can’t I just be more engaged?” or “Why does everything feel so effortful?” But that feels different from guilt, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m lying awake cataloging all the ways I’ve failed everyone.\n\nWorthlessness… no, I don’t think so. I don’t feel great about myself lately, but I can still sort of intellectually know I have value, even if I’m not feeling very connected to it emotionally. So I guess my answer is: not significantly, no. At least not in the way I understand those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little interest in activities, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two years. It feels like it’s been about eight months, give or take — that’s the period I keep coming back to when I try to put a timeline on it.\n\nThe change in my interest in things, and really the broader change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people, seems to have started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Before that, I had normal ups and downs, but it wasn’t this sustained thing. So I’d say it’s been months, not years — about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I felt low or discouraged for more than a couple of weeks, sure, but I don’t know if I would call it “depressed” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nLike, there have been stressful stretches where I was sad, worn down, not myself, but I don’t know that I can point to a clear episode and say, yes, that was definitely depression for more than two weeks. I tend to kind of keep functioning and second-guess whether what I’m feeling “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe, but I’m not confident. I don’t have a specific time that stands out to me as clearly being that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that with confidence. I mean, I’ve had different stretches in my life where I was down, or stressed, or sort of emotionally worn out, but when you ask “more than one period where you felt depressed,” I start wondering what threshold you mean.\n\nIf you mean, like, clear separate episodes where I would have recognized at the time, “I am depressed,” then… I’m not sure. I don’t have two distinct chapters in my mind that I can point to that way. It’s more like there have been times when life was hard and I didn’t feel like myself, but I don’t know whether those were really depression or just reactions to circumstances.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I can’t reliably identify more than one clear period. I’m hesitant to say yes when I’m not sure I’m using the word the way you are.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a previous episode of depression that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. I’m trying to think back, and I’ve definitely had times where I was unhappy or under a lot of strain for a couple weeks or longer, but whether that was a real “episode of depression” the way you’re asking… I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a prior period where I would say, yes, that was definitely depression for at least two weeks. I’ll think, well, I’m tired, I’m discouraged, things feel heavy—but then I’ll also tell myself there are reasons for feeling that way, or that maybe I’m just stressed. So before this period, I don’t think I had a firm sense that depression was what was happening. Maybe the thought crossed my mind vaguely, but I can’t say I really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression by a healthcare professional before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. I don’t remember a healthcare professional ever telling me, before this current period, “you have depression” or putting that diagnosis on it in a clear way.\n\nI’ve talked to people in healthcare settings before, but I don’t have a memory of anyone formally diagnosing me with depression. And I’m trying to be careful because there’s a difference between someone saying, you know, “you seem stressed” or asking about mood, versus actually diagnosing depression. So as far as I know, no—I haven’t been diagnosed with depression before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant depression before the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Before the past eight months… I don’t think I can point to anything that I would call significant depression, no. I tend to kind of keep functioning and second-guess whether what I’m feeling “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe, but I’m not confident. I don’t have a specific time that stands out to me as clearly being that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that with confidence. I mean, I’ve had different stretches in my life where I was down, or stressed, or sort of emotionally worn out, but when you ask “more than one period where you felt depressed,” I start wondering what threshold you mean.\n\nIf you mean, like, clear separate episodes where I would have recognized at the time, “I am depressed,” then… I’m not sure. I don’t have two distinct chapters in my mind that I can point to that way. It’s more like there have been times when life was hard and I didn’t feel like myself, but I don’t know whether those were really depression or just reactions to circumstances.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I can’t reliably identify more than one clear period. I’m hesitant to say yes when I’m not sure I’m using the word the way you are.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a previous episode of depression that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. I’m trying to think back, and I’ve definitely had times where I was unhappy or under a lot of strain for a couple weeks or longer, but whether that was a real “episode of depression” the way you’re asking… I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a prior period where I would say, yes, that was definitely depression for at least two weeks. It’s more that I can remember being worn down or discouraged at different points, but I’m hesitant to label it because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought you might be depressed before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve wondered at times whether I was just really unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember thinking, “I’m depressed,” like as a definite thing.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to analyze it too much. I’ll think, well, I’m tired, I’m discouraged, things feel heavy—but then I’ll also tell myself there are reasons for feeling that way, or that maybe I’m just stressed. So before this period, I don’t think I had a firm sense that depression was what was happening. Maybe the thought crossed my mind vaguely, but I can’t say I really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression by a healthcare professional before this period in your life?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. I don’t remember a healthcare professional ever telling me, before this current period, “you have depression” or putting that diagnosis on it in a clear way.\n\nI’ve talked to people in healthcare settings before, but I don’t have a memory of anyone formally diagnosing me with depression. And I’m trying to be careful because there’s a difference between someone saying, you know, “you seem stressed” or asking about mood, versus actually diagnosing depression. So as far as I know, no—I haven’t been diagnosed with depression before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant depression before the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Before the past eight months… I don’t think I can point to anything that I would call significant depression, no. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches in life, like anyone does, and sometimes I was very tired or discouraged or just kind of emotionally weighed down, but I don’t remember a distinct period before this where it felt clearly like a major depressive period.\n\nI’m trying to answer carefully because I don’t want to say “no” as if I’ve never had sadness or stress, but if you mean a period that really stood out as significant depression, before these last eight months, I’m not aware of one.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any previous episodes of depression that were treated and resolved before this current period started?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can’t remember any earlier episode where I was treated specifically for depression and then it resolved before this current period started.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I talked things through with people, or tried to get support in a general way, but not something I would describe as, “I had a depressive episode, it was treated, and then it got better.” That doesn’t really fit my memory of things. If there was anything like that, I don’t have a clear recollection of it, and I’d be hesitant to say yes just to fill in the blank. So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any previous episodes of depression that were similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in a way that I can clearly identify as similar to what I’m going through now.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard times before, and I can look back and say there were moments where I felt down or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember a previous episode that had the same quality or weight as this one. It’s not like I can say, “Oh yes, this happened five years ago and it was basically the same thing.” That doesn’t really come to mind.\n\nI’m trying not to overstate it, because sometimes when you’re in the middle of something, you start reinterpreting the past through the present, if that makes sense.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I would say mild, I think. I mean, I don’t want to minimize it, because it is definitely there and it affects how I feel day to day, but if you’re asking me to choose between mild, moderate, and severe, “mild” feels the most accurate.

It’s not nothing, but it also doesn’t feel like I’m completely swallowed by it, if that makes sense. I’m aware of it, and I can tell it’s weighing on me, but I wouldn’t honestly describe it as severe. So yes—mild is probably the best word.
